relative to the root system defining the algebra. This fact is best understood from the theory of weight systems and roots, but it would go beyond the scope of this work to review here. One point is worth emphasizing, however: there is no universally accepted ordering to the root systems of simple Lie algebras, and so Dynkin labels for irreducible representations may differ by permutations of their indices. When we are required to identify a representation using such labels, we make use of the conventions clarified in
appendix
B
.

What is important to us, however, is that some set of Casimirs will have independent eigenvalues on the subspaces of each irreducible representation of the Lie algebra. As such, they can be used to generate concrete projection tensors which connect arbitrary representations (
e.g.
tensor-product representations) into direct (outer) sums of particular subspaces of irreducible representations (possibly with multiplicity):

\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\simeq\!\!\bigoplus_{\text{irreps }\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}}\!\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}^{\oplus m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}}}}\,\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip-4.0pt:\hskip 1.25pt}$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,\bigoplus_{\text{irreps }\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}}\!\!m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(2.35)

That is, for each irreducible representation
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}
appearing in the decomposition of the arbitrary representation
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}
, there must exist tensors
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}^{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[s]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[s]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}
and
\mathbf{C}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[s]}}}
such that

\widehat{\mathbf{C}}^{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}).\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}.\mathbf{C}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}})=\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}\vskip-0.5pt

(2.36)

for each
\mu\!\in\!\{1,\ldots,m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}}}\}
.

Actually, the criterion in (
2.36
) is stronger than we’d like—as it requires that any rescaling of
\mathbf{C}_{\mu}\!\mapsto\!c\,\mathbf{C}_{\mu}
be compensated by a rescaling of
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}^{\mu}\!\mapsto\!c^{{-}1}\widehat{\mathbf{C}}^{\mu}
. We can allow for these tensors to be scaled independently by merely requiring that they satisfy, separately, the homogeneous conditions

\begin{split}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}.\mathbf{C}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}})&=\mathbf{C}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}).\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}\\
\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\text{and}\hskip 10.0pt$}}\widehat{\mathbf{C}}^{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}).\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}&=\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}.\widehat{\mathbf{C}}^{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}})\,.\end{split}\vskip-0.5pt

(2.37)

Given some particular, concrete representations
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}
and
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}
, how exactly can we construct the tensor
\mathbf{C}^{\mu}
? Notice that,
\mathbf{C}^{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[s]}}}
depends explicitly on the choice of bases made for these representations, and that similarity transformations on the representations
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}
and
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}s}}
fundamentally change the tensor. Let us now describe how they may be constructed. We follow mostly the construction described in
[
55
]
.

Suppose that one computes some Lie-algebra invariant
\Gamma{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}
such the one associated with
C_{2}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}})
, say, and discovers it
not
to be proportional to the identity. In particular, suppose that it has a collection of distinct eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\}
. Then we may define

\mathbf{P}_{\!i}\,\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}{-}\lambda_{j}}\big(\Gamma{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}{-}\lambda_{i}\,\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\big)\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(2.38)

These projectors satisfy (by construction) a completeness relation

\sum_{i}\mathbf{P}_{\!i}=\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\vskip-0.5pt

(2.39)

and—via the existence of an eigenspace decomposition and the spectral theorem—orthogonality

\mathbf{P}_{\!i}.\mathbf{P}_{\!j}=\delta_{i\,j}\mathbf{P}_{\!j}\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\hskip 10.0pt\text{(no sum)}$}}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(2.40)

Usefully, the rank of each projector is given by its trace:

\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{P}_{\!i})=\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{P}_{\!i})<\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}})\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(2.41)

The
\mathbf{P}_{\!i}
are called projectors because they literally project onto subspaces of the
\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}})
space with definite eigenvalues for the operator encoded by
\Gamma
. To be clear, they are still
\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}})\!\times\!\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}})
matrices, but they are of lower rank.

Now, according to the defining property of any (not necessarily irreducible) representation (
2.1
), because
\mathbf{P}_{\!i}.\mathbf{P}_{\!i}\!=\!\mathbf{P}_{\!i}
for any
i
, it is easy to see that whatever commutation relations are satisfied by
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}
will also be satisfied by

(\mathbf{P}_{\!i}.\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}.\mathbf{P}_{\!i}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\vskip-0.5pt

(2.42)

for each
i
. That is, from the reducible representation
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}
we may construct the more reduced, lower-dimensional representations associated with the generators given in (
2.42
).

As the rank of the matrix
\mathbf{P}_{i}
is less than
\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}})
, we can always write it in terms of matrices of full rank via

\mathbf{P}_{\!i}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}{}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip-4.0pt:\hskip 1.25pt}$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}a}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[a]}}\mathbf{{C}}_{i}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}{}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}a}}}\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{i}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}a}}}{}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}a}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\,,\vskip-0.5pt

(2.43)

(say, by taking the first
\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{P}_{i})
independent rows or columns) where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[a]}
now only runs over the range
\{1,\ldots,\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{P}_{\!i})\}
of independent rows or columns. Moreover, the property that
\mathbf{P}_{\!i}.\mathbf{P}_{\!i}\!=\!\mathbf{P}_{\!i}
readily implies that

\widehat{\mathbf{{C}}}_{i}.\mathbf{C}_{i}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}\widehat{\mathbf{{C}}}_{i}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[a]}}}{}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[a]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}}\mathbf{C}_{i}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}}{}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[a]}}}=\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[a]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[a]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[a]}}}\,\vskip-0.5pt

(2.44)

from which it is easy to see that the particular rank-three tensor

(\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{i}.\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}.\mathbf{C}_{i}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[a]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[a]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[a]}}}\vskip-0.5pt

(2.45)

will also satisfy the same commutation relations as
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}
, but now involving generators of size
\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{P}_{\!i})\!\times\!\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{P}_{\!i})
.

To be clear, the partitioning of
\mathbf{P}_{\!i}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip-4.0pt:\hskip 1.25pt}$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,\mathbf{C}_{i}.\widehat{\mathbf{{C}}}_{i}
is only well-defined up to a similarity transform: this pairing is indistinguishable from

(\mathbf{C}_{i},\mathbf{\overline{C}}_{i})\mapsto(\mathbf{C}_{i}.\mathbf{M},\mathbf{\overline{M}}.\widehat{\mathbf{{C}}}_{i})\,,\vskip-0.5pt

(2.46)

for any invertible matrix
\mathbf{M}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[a]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[a]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[a]}}}
. This is of course identical to the fact that any rank-three tensor related to (
2.45
) by similarity will also constitute a representation with identical structure constants.

By virtue of the existence of a set of Casimirs sufficient to identify all irreducible representations, there exist projectors (or sequences thereof) by which the specific projectors (
2.36
) can be constructed. Moreover, provided some ‘standard’ (
\mathrm{GL}(\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}a}}))
-fixed) representation matrices have been chosen for each irreducible representation, the only ambiguity left in the determination of the projectors
(\widehat{\mathbf{C}},\mathbf{C})
is the overall scaling by some constant:
(\widehat{\mathbf{C}},\mathbf{C})\mapsto(c\,\widehat{\mathbf{C}},c^{{-}1}\mathbf{C})
for any constant
c
.

2.2
Tensor Products and Clebsch-Gordan Coefficients

Given any two representations
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}
and
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}
, one may define the
tensor product
representation denoted ‘
(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}})
’ via

(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}\,\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}+\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(2.47)

Via the canonical isomorphism
\mathbb{V}^{\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}})}\!\otimes\!\mathbb{V}^{\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}})}\mapsto\mathbb{V}^{\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}})\times\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}})}
, it is common to view the tensor product as defined over the
\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}})\!\times\!\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}})
-dimensional space of tuples of indices
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}\simeq[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r},\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}]
; although according to our definition of a
matrix representation
in (
2.1
) this would be required, we find that the partitioned sets of indices are more convenient for what follows.

The decomposition of the tensor product representation into irreducible representations of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is accomplished via what are known as
Clebsch-Gordan coefficients
. These can be constructed in the same way as above for the decomposition of arbitrary (not necessarily irreducible) representations. That is, we may construct a pair of tensors
\mathbf{{C}}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}})
and
\widehat{\mathbf{{C}}}^{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}})
which decomposes the tensor product into the irreducible representation
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}
according to

\widehat{\mathbf{C}}^{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}).(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}).\mathbf{{C}}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}})=\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}\quad\text{for each $\mu$,}\vskip-0.5pt

(2.48)

where, as always
\mu
runs over the multiplicity
\mu\!\in\!\{1,\ldots,m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}}}\}
, using the shorthand
m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}}}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\otimes\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\otimes\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}}}
.

As before, the relation (
2.48
) is invariant under
(\widehat{\mathbf{C}},\mathbf{C})\mapsto(c\,\widehat{\mathbf{C}},c^{{-}1}\mathbf{C})
for any constant
c
; that is, while the overall scaling of
\mathbf{C}
may be arbitrarily chosen, that of
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}
cannot be independently adjusted. It turns out to be useful to allow for independent scaling choices, which is easily accomplished by considering that these tensors be instead defined
merely
by the homogeneous relations:

\begin{split}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}).\mathbf{{C}}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}})&\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,\mathbf{{C}}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}).\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}\,;\\
\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{\overline{C}}^{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}).(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}})&\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}.\mathbf{\overline{C}}^{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}})\,.\end{split}\vskip-0.5pt

(2.49)

These relations allow us to choose any overall scale we’d like separately for
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{\overline{C}}
. Our convention here is that
\mathbf{\overline{C}}
should in fact be
defined
by swapping incoming and outgoing representations with their conjugates:

\widehat{\mathbf{C}}^{\mu}\,\propto\,\mathbf{\overline{C}}^{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[t]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[t]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{r}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[\overline{r}]},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\overline{s}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[\overline{s}]}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{t}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\overline{t}]}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{{C}}^{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{R}}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\overline{S}}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{t}}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\overline{s}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\overline{s}}}}\smash{\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{t}}}}\,\,\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{}}_{\phantom{\smash{}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\,\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{}}_{\phantom{\smash{}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\overline{s}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}\,\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{t}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[t]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{t}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[t]}}}\smash{}}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(2.50)

Of course,
\mathbf{\overline{C}}\propto\widehat{\mathbf{C}}
, but these need not be identical.

Importantly (and independently of the scales chosen for these tensors), we may always choose Clebsch-Gordan tensors to be orthogonal with respect to their multiplicities. Specifically, we may always arrange them so that

\big\langle\mathbf{\overline{C}}^{\nu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}})|\mathbf{{C}}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}})\big\rangle\,\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}\in{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[t]}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{\overline{C}}^{\nu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}}}\mathbf{{C}}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}}\propto\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\nu}}_{\phantom{\smash{\nu}}\smash{\mu}}\,.\vskip-5.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(2.51)

(Here, the constant of proportionality can depend on the index
\mu
.)

In many cases of interest to us below, there are no multiplicities to consider; and the few instances where multiplicities do matter, independent orthogonal Clebsch-Gordan tensors can be constructed by decomposing tensor-powers into symmetric or antisymmetric parts. For example, for
\mathfrak{a}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}>1}
Lie algebras, the multiplicity
m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad\,ad}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad\,ad}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}}}{=}2
, which can be cleanly separated into one copy arising each from the symmetric and antisymmetric tensor products.

Because of the importance of
\mathfrak{a}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}>1}
for physics, it may be worth clarifying how this works in somewhat greater detail. Whenever one considers the tensor product representations of two identical representations, it is worthwhile to exploit the canonical isomorphism between
\mathbb{V}^{\mathrm{d}}\!\otimes\!\mathbb{V}^{\mathrm{d}}\mapsto\mathbb{V}^{\mathrm{d}\times\mathrm{d}}\mapsto\mathbb{V}^{\mathrm{d}}\!\wedge\!\mathbb{V}^{\mathrm{d}}\!\oplus\!\mathrm{Sym}\big(\mathbb{V}^{\mathrm{d}},\mathbb{V}^{\mathrm{d}}\big)
; in terms of this, we may view the Lie algebra tensor product representation as given by the outer sum of

\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip-4.0pt:\hskip 1.25pt}$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\wedge\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}})\oplus(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\odot\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}})\,,\vskip-0.5pt

(2.52)

where we have used ‘
\wedge
’ and ‘
\odot
’ to denote the image of the tensor product on the
antisymmetric
and
symmetric
product spaces, respectively. In the case of the adjoint representation
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}
of
\mathfrak{a}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}>1}
, each of these tensor-products includes in its decomposition a single copy of
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}
, allowing us to define the two Clebsches
\mathbf{C}_{[\mu]}
according to

\begin{split}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}\!\wedge\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}).\mathbf{C}_{1}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}})&=\,\mathbf{C}_{1}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}).\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}\,,\\
(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}\!\odot\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}).\mathbf{C}_{2}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}})&=\,\mathbf{C}_{2}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}).\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}\,,\end{split}\vskip-0.5pt

(2.53)

As we will clarify in more detail below, it turns out that the first of these can always be taken to be identical to the adjoint representation itself

\mathbf{C}_{1}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}})\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}={\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\;\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/f_as_clebsch_1}}}$}\,,\vskip-4.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(2.54)

while the second can be chosen to be related to the so-called ‘
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}d}
tensor’

\mathbf{C}_{2}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}})\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}d}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}\in{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}d}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]a}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]a}}}\smash{}}g^{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}}_{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a\,[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\;\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/d_tensor_diagram}}}$}\,,\vskip-4.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(2.55)

where
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}d}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{}}
is the fully symmetric tensor typically defined in terms of the fundamental representation ‘
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}F}}
’ analogously to the antisymmetric tensor
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{}}
in (
2.31
):

{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}d}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{}}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}F}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1\,2\,3})+\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}F}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2\,1\,3})\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(2.56)

It is easy to see that these tensors
\mathbf{C}_{\mu}
are automatically orthogonal.

Unfortunately, this trick of symmetrization/anti-symmetrization does not always suffice; in particular, this doesn’t work for cases where
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}
and
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}
are distinct. For example, in the case of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{a}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}3}}
we find that the tensor product of the irreducible representations of dimension
\mathbf{{384}}
and
\mathbf{{729}}
includes
seven
copies of the irreducible representation of dimension
\mathbf{{960}}
:

\hbox{\hskip 8.73624pt\hskip-8.62495pt\hbox{$\text{\rule[-5.0pt]{0.0pt}{10.0pt}{\normalsize$\mathbf{384}$}}$}\hskip-8.62495pt\hskip-8.73624pt\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox{$\rule[-4.0pt]{0.0pt}{10.0pt}\text{{\footnotesize$[131]$}}$}}\hskip-8.73624pt\hskip 8.73624pt}\!\otimes\!\hbox{\hskip 8.73624pt\hskip-8.62495pt\hbox{$\text{\rule[-5.0pt]{0.0pt}{10.0pt}{\normalsize$\mathbf{729}$}}$}\hskip-8.62495pt\hskip-8.73624pt\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox{$\rule[-4.0pt]{0.0pt}{10.0pt}\text{{\footnotesize$[222]$}}$}}\hskip-8.73624pt\hskip 8.73624pt}\supset\hbox{\hskip 8.73624pt\hskip-8.62495pt\hbox{$\text{\rule[-5.0pt]{0.0pt}{10.0pt}{\normalsize$\mathbf{960}$}}$}\hskip-8.62495pt\hskip-8.73624pt\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox{$\rule[-4.0pt]{0.0pt}{10.0pt}\text{{\footnotesize$[313]$}}$}}\hskip-8.73624pt\hskip 8.73624pt}^{\oplus 7}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(2.57)

(Here, we have indicated the Dynkin labels for each of the irreducible representations; however, in this case, such labelling is superfluous as these are the unique irreducible representations of
\mathfrak{a}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}3}}
of these dimensions.)

In many cases like that of (
2.57
), the construction of orthogonal Clebsch-Gordan tensors is highly non-trivial and computationally cumbersome. Conveniently, however, these cases rarely arise in situations that are most interesting and relevant to physics. When there are no multiplicities greater than one appearing in a tensor product, we often suppress the ‘
\mu
’ index label, referring only to ‘the’ Clebsch-Gordan tensor—as opposed to one among many. And for cases such as the
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
versus
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}d}
tensors relevant (only) to
\mathfrak{a}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}>1}
, we use the diagrammatic notation introduced above to uniquely identify each Clebsch.

2.3
Universality of Clebsch-Gordan Colour Tensors

Although such Clebsch-Gordan coefficients may seem quite removed from the more obvious tensors such as
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}
,
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}
, or even
g_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}
, it turns out that all of these more familiar tensors can be interpreted as Clebsch-Gordan coefficients. For example, for any representation
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}
, the tensor product representation
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}

always
includes at least one copy of
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}
; and it is always possible to choose the normalization of our Clebsches so that

\mathbf{C}_{1}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\,\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(2.58)

To be clear, if
m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}}\!>\!1
, there will be other Clebsch-Gordan coefficients as well. This happens, for example, for
\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}})
in the case of
\mathfrak{a}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}>1}
gauge-theory. (Another example is the
\mathbf{350}
-dimensional representation of
\mathfrak{d}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}4}}
, which has multiplicity with the adjoint of 3.) Regardless of multiplicity, however, there is always one instance that can be identified by (
2.58
). In such cases, we choose to suppress its multiplicity index and need not further embellish our diagrammatic notation.

All the examples involving representation tensors appearing in this work so far can therefore be immediately interpreted as those involving (particular, if multiple exist) Clebsch-Gordan coefficients. This allows us to directly re-interpret the many various colour tensors constructed out of generators as those constructed from Clebsch-Gordan coefficients, unifying the various diagrammatic notations used for colour tensors in the literature.

Another category of tensors we’ve encountered before are the rank-two tensors relating incoming representation indices to outgoing conjugate-representation indices and the ‘metric’ tensors for real representations (such as the Killing metric for the adjoint representation). These too can be understood as examples of Clebsch-Gordan coefficients: those for the projection of a pair of representations into the trivial representation, denoted ‘
\mathbf{1}
’.

Recall that for every representation
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}
of every simple Lie algebra, there is a unique conjugate representation
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{R}}}
defined (up to similarity) by the condition that
m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R\,\overline{R}}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R\,\overline{R}}}}}\smash{\mathbf{1}}}\!=\!1
. That is, there always exists a set of Clebsch-Gordan coefficients
\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{R}}}|\mathbf{1})
which we may conventionally take to be given by the identity:

\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{R}}}|\mathbf{1}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]\,[\overline{r}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]\,[\overline{r}]}}}\smash{1}}\,\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]\,[\overline{r}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]\,[\overline{r}]}}}\smash{1}}=\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]\,[\overline{r}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]\,[\overline{r}]}}}\smash{\;}}\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\raisebox{9.125pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/adjoint_vertex_as_one_clebsch}}}$}\;\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\;\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/d_tensor_as_another_clebsch}}}$}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(2.59)

Notice this is consistent with our convention outlined in (
2.23
). To be clear, for
real
representations
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\simeq\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{R}}}
, there is often a non-trivial similarity transformation between the coordinates indexed by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[\overline{r}]}
—which are effectively
defined
by (
2.59
)—and those indexed by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}
; in such cases, there will be some non-trivial metric tensor involved. For the adjoint representation, for example this would be the Killing metric

\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}{ad}}}|\mathbf{1}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{1}}\,\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,g_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}\;\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\raisebox{9.125pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/metric_as_RRbar1_clebsch}}}$}\;\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\;\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/RRbar_vertex}}}$}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(2.60)

To be clear, we still would
define

\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{ad}}}|\mathbf{1}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\overline{\mathfrak{g}}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\overline{\mathfrak{g}}]}}}\smash{1}}\,\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,\delta^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\overline{\mathfrak{g}}]}}}\;\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\raisebox{9.125pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/killing_metric_as_clebsch}}}$}\;\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/adj_adjbar_vertex}}}$}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/adj_propagator}}}$}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

The discussion above makes it clear that all of the tensors (and diagrammatics) we have encountered above can be universally understood as involving Clebsches—provided that their normalizations are chosen judiciously according to (
2.58
) and (
2.59
). This is good, because we would like to compare the more familiar colour tensors used by physicists in the representations of amplitudes to the more novel constructions discussed below.

2.4
Conjugation, Contraction, Vacuum Graphs, and Orthogonality

We have seen many examples above where tensors are contracted by summing over internal indices. Generally, for any pair of colour tensors
C_{1},C_{2}
involving some representation
\mathbf{Q}
outgoing and incoming, respectively, we may define their contraction via

\begin{split}~\\[0.0pt]
\hskip-16.0pt\Bigg\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}C_{1}(\text{\makebox[10.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\cdots|\text{\makebox[10.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\cdots\text{\makebox[10.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\,\cdots\,}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\,\cdots\,}}\smash{[q]\,\cdots\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[u]}}},\\
C_{2}(\text{\makebox[10.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\cdots\text{\makebox[10.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}|\text{\makebox[10.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\cdots){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{[q]\,\cdots\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{[q]\,\cdots\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[t]}\,\cdots}}\end{array}\Bigg\}&\hskip-4.0pt\mapsto\!D(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\,\cdots\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\,\cdots\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[t]}\,\cdots\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[u]}}}\\[-5.0pt]
\hskip 20.0pt&\hskip 10.0pt\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\!\!\sum_{q\in[q]}C_{1}(\cdots|\mathbf{Q}\cdots){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\,\cdots\,}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\,\cdots\,}}\smash{q\,\cdots\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[u]}}}C_{2}(\mathbf{Q}\cdots|\cdots){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{q\,\cdots\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{q\,\cdots\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[t]}\,\cdots}}\hskip-40.0pt\\[-8.0pt]
\end{split}\vskip-0.5pt

(2.61)

which we may represent graphically via

\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 10.0pt$}}\Bigg\{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/left_vertex_merger}}}$},\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/right_vertex_merger}}}$}\Bigg\}\!\mapsto\!\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/merged_graph_example}}}$}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(2.62)

In such cases, it is easy to see that the
particular
tensors involved in the representation
\mathbf{Q}
are not important, as any similarity transform would leave the summand invariant (see equation (
2.16
)). In particular,
if the representation
\mathbf{Q}
were self-conjugate
, then as
\mathbf{Q}\!\simeq\!\overline{\mathbf{Q}}
, this tensor would be identical to that with the orientation of
\mathbf{Q}
reversed. If
\mathbf{Q}\!\nsim\!\overline{\mathbf{Q}}
, however, this information must be preserved. For the sake of clarification, among the four possible choices,

=

\neq

=

(2.63)

only the first and last pair are equal to each other; each pair encodes a generally distinct tensor. Always remember that reversing an arrow is identical (by
definition
) to exchanging a representation with its conjugate.

Although the resulting tensor is independent of the particular basis choice for coordinates for the tensor
\mathbf{Q}
, it is clear that the new tensor
D
still carries
some
information about the internal representation
\mathbf{Q}
. This information may be preserved in the graph by labeling the representations associated with each edge, or by indicating the representation in some other way.

This residual information on the internal representation is especially evident for a very important class of colour ‘tensors’:
vacuum graphs
. These are tensors of zero rank; they are simply representation-theoretic ‘constants’ defined precisely by the representations involved in their definition, and the graphs in which they are sewn together.

Perhaps the simplest vacuum graph can be obtained from the ‘propagator’ of any representation

\delta^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{[r]\,}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\;\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/generic_propagator}}}$}\;\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\raisebox{-6.175pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/label_down_propagator}}}$}\,\vskip-0.5pt

(2.64)

via contracting its indices:

\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip-20.0pt\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}})\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}|=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}\delta^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}_{\phantom{r\,}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}\;\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/rep_self_trace}}}$}=\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/rep_self_trace_bar}}}$}\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{r}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[\overline{r}]}}\delta_{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{r}}}}^{\smash{\phantom{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{r}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{r}}}}=|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[\overline{r}]}|=\mathrm{dim}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{\overline{R}}}).\hskip-150.0pt

(2.65)

Here, it is clear that this constant depends only upon the size of the representation, and not upon the
GL(\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{R}))
representative chosen for its particular components.

Another familiar example of a vacuum graph involves only the vertex defining any particular representation:

\Theta(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{ad}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}})\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\;\Leftrightarrow\;$}}\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/theta_rrg_v1}}}$}=\hskip-3.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/theta_rrg_v2}}}$}\vskip-0.5pt

(2.66)

We may compute this representation-dependent number by cutting open any edge. In particular, by cutting an
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}
edge and using the definition of the quadratic Casimir (
2.34
), or cutting the adjoint edge and using the definition of the Dynkin index defined in (
2.29
) we find the well-known result that

\Theta(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{ad}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}})=C_{2}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}})\,\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}})=T(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}})\,\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}g}})\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(2.67)

Alternatively, we could imagine inserting internal conjugations, considering
\Theta(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}})
as given by

\Theta(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{ad}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}})\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\;\Leftrightarrow\;$}}\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/theta_rrg_v1}}}$}=\hskip-3.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/theta_rrg_v3}}}$}\vskip-0.5pt

(2.68)

from which we can recognize the complete contraction of indices between the
conjugate
tensors
\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}}
and

\begin{split}\mathbf{C}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{\mathbf{{r}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{\mathbf{{ad}}}}|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{\mathbf{{r}}}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[\overline{r}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\overline{\mathfrak{g}}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[\overline{r}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\overline{\mathfrak{g}}]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[\overline{r}]}}}&\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{i}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}\in{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}\end{subarray}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[\overline{r}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{1}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[\overline{r}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{1}}}}\smash{}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\overline{\mathfrak{g}}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\overline{\mathfrak{g}}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}}}\smash{}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{}}_{\phantom{\smash{}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[\overline{r}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{2}}}}\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{2}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{2}}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{1}}}}\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{i}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}{a}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[{\mathfrak{g}}]}\end{subarray}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[\overline{r}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{1}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[\overline{r}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{1}}}}\smash{}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\overline{\mathfrak{g}}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\overline{\mathfrak{g}}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}}}\smash{}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{}}_{\phantom{\smash{}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[\overline{r}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{2}}}}\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{ad}}}|\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{2}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{a}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{2}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{a}}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{1}}}}\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{i}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}{a_{i}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[{\mathfrak{g}}]}\end{subarray}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[\overline{r}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{1}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[\overline{r}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{1}}}}\smash{}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\overline{\mathfrak{g}}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\overline{\mathfrak{g}}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}}}}\smash{}}g^{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}}_{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}\,a_{2}}}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{}}_{\phantom{\smash{}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[\overline{r}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{2}}}}\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}{ad}}}|\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{2}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}{a_{2}}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{2}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}{a_{2}}}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{1}}}}\,.\end{split}\vskip-0.5pt

(2.69)

In general, it is useful to define the
conjugate
tensor to any tensor via

\begin{split}\overline{C(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[t]}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[u]}}}}\,&\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}C(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[u]}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[t]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[u]}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[t]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\\
&\hskip-150.0pt=\hskip-7.0pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{r}}\in\text{\makebox[7.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[\overline{r}]}\hskip-150.0pt$}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\overline{s}}\in\text{\makebox[7.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[\overline{s}]}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\hskip-10.0pt\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\overline{t}}\in\text{\makebox[7.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[\overline{t}]}\hskip-150.0pt$}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{u}}\in\text{\makebox[7.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\overline{u}]}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\hskip-10.0pt\end{subarray}}\hskip-10.0pt\big(\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[u]}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{u}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[u]}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{u}}}}\smash{}}\!\cdots\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[t]}{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\overline{t}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[t]}{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\overline{t}}}}\smash{}}\big)\big(\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{}}_{\phantom{\smash{}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\overline{s}}}}\!\cdots\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{}}_{\phantom{\smash{}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{r}}}}\big)C(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{R}}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\overline{S}}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\overline{T}}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{U}}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{r}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\overline{s}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{r}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\overline{s}}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\overline{t}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{u}}}}.\end{split}\vskip-0.5pt

(2.70)

Graphically, this corresponds to flipping the diagram, and reversing the orientation of all edges (including those internal to the diagram). For any pair of tensors

C(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}})\quad\text{and}\quad C^{\prime}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}})\vskip-0.5pt

(2.71)

we can define the
complete contraction
between them using a bra/ket-like notation:

\langle C^{\prime}|C\rangle\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\hskip-7.0pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\makebox[5.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}r}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\in\text{\makebox[7.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[r]}\hskip-150.0pt$}},\ldots,\text{\makebox[5.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\in\text{\makebox[7.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\hskip-10.0pt\\
\text{\makebox[5.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}t}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\in\text{\makebox[7.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[t]}\hskip-150.0pt$}},\ldots,\text{\makebox[5.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}u}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\in\text{\makebox[7.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[u]}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\hskip-10.0pt\end{subarray}}\hskip-10.0ptC^{\prime}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}u}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}t}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}u}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}t}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}}C(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}t}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}u}}}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(2.72)

The orthogonality condition of Clebsch-Gordan coefficients (
2.51
) is one of many examples where
all
the representations’ indices have been contracted, leaving only a residual dependence on the discrete data of the representations involved and the multiplicity indices
\mu
required to define the particular Clebsch-Gordan tensor. If we let

\hskip-55.0pt\Theta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\nu}}_{\phantom{\smash{\nu}}\smash{\mu}}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}T}})\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\big\langle\mathbf{\overline{C}}^{\nu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}T}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}})|\mathbf{{C}}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}T}})\big\rangle\,\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\hskip-8.0pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}\in{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[t]}\end{subarray}}\hskip-8.0pt\mathbf{\overline{C}}^{\nu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}T}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}}}\mathbf{{C}}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}T}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}t}}}\hskip-40.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(2.73)

which we may represent graphically as

\Theta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\nu}}_{\phantom{\smash{\nu}}\smash{\mu}}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}T}})\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\;\Leftrightarrow\;$}}\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/theta_graph_general}}}$}\vskip-0.5pt

(2.74)

then orthogonality is the requirement that

\Theta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\nu}}_{\phantom{\smash{\nu}}\smash{\mu}}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}T}})\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip-4.0pt:\hskip 1.25pt}$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,\Theta_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}})\,\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\nu}}_{\phantom{\smash{\nu}}\smash{\mu}}\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\hskip 10.0pt\text{(no sum)\,.}$}}\vskip-0.5pt

(2.75)

(As always, the constant of proportionality can depend on the discrete data labeling the vacuum graph.)

Colour-Orthogonality Among Sets of Colour Graphs

Recall that the colour tensor bases described here are constructed from tensors of Clebsch-Gordan coefficients arranged according to some fixed tree; for example,

\mathcal{B}_{\mu\nu\cdots\rho\sigma}^{\hskip 4.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{R}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{S}}\cdots\!|\!\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{T}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{U}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}\cdots}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}\cdots}}\smash{\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[t]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[u]}}}\;\;\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\;\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/graphical_diagram_for_basis}}}$}\,.\vskip-5.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(2.76)

These basis tensors are labelled by the internal irreducible representations
\mathbf{a},\ldots,\mathbf{c}
and the particular Clebsch-Gordan tensors chosen for each triple—indicated by the discrete indices
\mu,\nu,\ldots,\rho,\sigma
. These tensors are defined in the obvious way in terms of three-representation-dependent Clebsch-Gordan tensors, constructed recursively via

\mathcal{B}_{\mu\nu\cdots\sigma}^{\hskip 2.5pt\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}}\mathbf{b}\cdots}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{R}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{S}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{T}}\cdots\!|\!\cdots)\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}\in{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[a]}}C_{\mu}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{R}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}})^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{[r][s]}}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}}\mathcal{B}^{\hskip 1.5pt\smash{\mathbf{b}\cdots}}_{\nu\cdots\sigma}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{a}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{T}}\cdots|\!\cdots)^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[t]}\cdots}}_{\phantom{\smash{a[t]\cdots}}\cdots}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(2.77)

For each such colour tensor, there is a natural dual tensor involving conjugated representations incoming and outgoing, or—equivalently—involving incoming and outgoing representations reversed:

\begin{split}\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\mu\nu\cdots\rho\sigma}^{\hskip 4.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip-4.0pt:\hskip 1.25pt}$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,\mathcal{B}^{\smash{\mu\nu\cdots\rho\sigma}}_{\hskip 4.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}\;\;\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\;\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/dual_basis_direct}}}$}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/dual_basis_flipped}}}$}\,.\end{split}\vskip-0.5pt

(2.78)

We prove in section
3.1
that these tensors are
automatically orthogonal
(provided the building-block, three-representation Clebsch-Gordan coefficients have been orthogonalized in their multiplicity indices
\mu
). That is, letting the complete contraction between colour tensors involving conjugated representations be denoted
\langle C|D\rangle
, we have

\langle\mathcal{B}^{\smash{\mu^{\prime}\!\cdots\sigma^{\prime}}}_{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a^{\prime}}\!\cdots\mathbf{c^{\prime}}}|\mathcal{B}_{\mu\cdots\sigma}^{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}\rangle\propto\big(\delta^{\mu^{\prime}}_{\mu}\cdots\delta^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{\sigma}\big)\big(\delta^{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathbf{a^{\prime}}}\cdots\delta^{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathbf{c^{\prime}}}\big)\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(2.79)

Moreover, the same orthogonality is ensured for any basis constructed using
any

fixed
trivalent tree of Clebsch-Gordan coefficients connecting the external particles’ colour representations via the exchange of intermediate irreducible representations. The choice of how the external representations are arranged on the external legs of the tree is also arbitrary (but fixed)—and this choice can be viewed as part of the characteristic defining the tree graph itself and the corresponding basis of colour tensors.

Due to this orthogonality, the decomposition of any particular colour tensor into this basis can be easily achieved via

C({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{R}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{S}}\cdots\!|\!\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{T}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{U}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}\\
\mu\cdots\sigma\end{subarray}}\frac{\langle\mathcal{B}^{\smash{\mu\cdots\sigma}}_{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}|C\rangle}{\langle\mathcal{B}^{\smash{\mu\cdots\sigma}}_{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}|\mathcal{B}_{\mu\cdots\sigma}^{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}\rangle}\mathcal{B}_{\mu\cdots\sigma}^{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{R}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{S}}\cdots\!|\!\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{T}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{U}})\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(2.80)

As we describe below, such a decomposition can also be implemented graphically and recursively for any colour tensor constructed from representation matrices, colour ‘traces’, structure constants, or other graphs of Clebsch-Gordan coefficients.

3
Clebsch-Gordan Colour Tensor Bases

As discussed at length in ref.
[
45
]
, the number of independent colour tensors involving arbitrary external particle representations is given by

\mathrm{rank}\Big[\mathrm{span}\{C(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}})\}\Big]=m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{R}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{S}}\otimes{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{\overline{T}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{U}}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{R}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{S}}\otimes{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{\overline{T}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{U}}}}}\smash{\mathbf{1}}}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(3.1)

This number can be computed recursively, by decomposing arbitrary pairs of external representations into irreducible representations of the Lie algebra. This can be done in many inequivalent ways due to the associativity of the tensor product and the commutativity of this counting. Any sequence of pairwise decompositions can be viewed as encoding a trivalent tree with vertices connecting external representations to each other via internal, irreducible representations. By viewing the vertices of such a diagram as Clebsch-Gordan coefficients for the decomposition of arbitrary products into irreducible representations, we construct a spanning set of explicit colour tensors manifestly of full rank. Importantly, the resulting basis of colour tensors consists of tensors which
share the same underlying tree
with a
fixed
ordering of external particle representations.

There are of course many trivalent tree graphs involving
n
external edges; and for each of these, there are many distinct arrangements of possible external representations. If all the external edges represent distinct representations, then there will be
(2n{-}5)!!
different choices of trees connecting the fixed (but arbitrarily arranged) external representations.

One word of caution is in order about how the external particle representations can be ordered ‘distinctly’ in such a graph. Although when all the external particle representations involved are distinct it is hard to overlook that each Clebsch-Gordan coefficient requires a
precise
ordering of its arguments—as
C(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}})\!\neq\!C(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}})
as tensors—one must be careful, when
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\simeq\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}
. For example, when encountering a Clebsch-Gordan coefficient such as
C_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}})
, one must be careful to remember that these tensors need not enjoy any symmetry under the exchange of the first two representations:

\Big\{C_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{1}r_{2}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{1}r_{2}}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}t}}}\Big\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{i}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\\
\hskip 2.95pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}t}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[t]}\end{subarray}}\neq\Big\{C_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{2}r_{1}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{2}r_{1}}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}t}}}\Big\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{i}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\\
\hskip 2.95pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}t}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[t]}\end{subarray}}\;\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/rrt_clebsch_perm_1}}}$}\neq\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/rrt_clebsch_perm_2}}}$}\,.\vskip-15.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

Thus, one must view the ordering of (especially isomorphic) representation labels as being important to the meaning of the resulting tensor. This issue is effectively avoided through our convention that the sequence of arguments of the tensor should read from the bottom-left of the diagram, proceeding clockwise around the graph; but this convention makes some graphs—trace tensors defined with arbitrary orderings, for example—extremely cumbersome to draw. In such cases, we often prefer to eschew diagrammatic notation all-together, or otherwise view the sequence-ordering of arguments as additional information adorning the tensor diagram.

In general, completeness ensures that

\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/rrt_clebsch_perm_2}}}$}\in\mathrm{span}_{\nu}\left\{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/rrt_clebsch_perm_1prime}}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\right\};\vskip-15.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(3.2)

which is to say that there must exist some coefficients
\mathscr{R}^{\smash{\phantom{\mu}\nu}}_{\smash{\mu}}
such that

\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/rrt_clebsch_perm_2}}}$}\,\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip-4.0pt:\hskip 1.25pt}$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,\sum_{\nu\in[m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}}}]}\hskip-4.0pt\mathscr{R}^{\smash{\phantom{\mu}\nu}}_{\smash{\mu}}\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/rrt_clebsch_perm_1prime}}}$}\,.\vskip-15.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(3.3)

To be clear, the above statement is independent of the representation
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}
(which itself may or may not be isomorphic to
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}
).

It will be useful later to extend the notion of transposing tensor arguments to the general case, letting

{C}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[t]}}}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip-4.0pt:\hskip 1.25pt}$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\sum_{\nu\in[m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}}}]}\hskip-5.0pt\mathscr{R}^{\phantom{\mu}\nu}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}})C_{\nu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[t]}}}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(3.4)

Of course, when
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\nsim\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}
, this operation is fairly trivial.

3.1
Bases of Colour Tensors for Arbitrary Coloured Particles

The generalization of the discussion above to the case of an arbitrary collection of external representations should be clear: any particular, fixed, trivalent tree constructed from Clebsch-Gordan tensors connecting the external representations via internal, irreducible representations will form a complete basis of colour tensors. Although many of the key ideas have been outlined above, let us briefly sketch the essential arguments involved in proving the non-perturbative completeness and colour-orthogonality of the tensors constructed in this way.

Consider, for example, the case of 4 particles with distinct representations. There are three choices of graph and correspondingly three choices of colour tensor bases constructed from Clebsch-Gordan coefficients:

\begin{array}[]{c@{$\;\;\;\;\;\;$}c@{$\;\;\;\;\;\;$}c}\mathcal{B}^{\hskip 2.2pt\mathbf{{a}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\mu\nu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}})&\mathcal{C}^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathbf{{b}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\rho\sigma}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}})&\mathcal{D}^{\hskip 2.2pt\mathbf{{c}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\tau\upsilon}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}})\\
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/four_point_generic_basis_s}}}$}&\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/four_point_generic_basis_t}}}$}&\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/four_point_gneric_basis_u}}}$}\end{array}\vskip-0.5pt

(3.5)

In each case, the individual tensors are labelled by a single irreducible representation and a pair of multiplicities; but
which
particular irreducible representations appear, how many appear, and the multiplicity-ranges for each pair of Clebsches can be very different for each. Only their total number (and their total linear-spans as tensors) must agree.

It is not hard to see that each of these bases are individually self-orthogonal. To see this, one may compute the overlaps:

\begin{array}[]{c@{$\!$}c@{$\!$}c}\langle\mathcal{B}_{\hskip 1.5pt\mathbf{{b}}}^{\hskip 0.0pt\rho\sigma}|\mathcal{B}^{\hskip 2.2pt\mathbf{{a}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\mu\nu}\rangle&\langle\mathcal{C}_{\hskip 2.2pt\mathbf{{a}}}^{\hskip-1.0pt\mu\nu}|\mathcal{C}^{\hskip 1.2pt\mathbf{{b}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\rho\sigma}\rangle&\langle\mathcal{D}_{\hskip 2.2pt\mathbf{{a}}}^{\hskip 0.0pt\mu\nu}|\mathcal{D}^{\hskip 1.2pt\mathbf{{c}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\tau\upsilon}\rangle\\
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/generic_s_basis_self_contraction}}}$}&\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/generic_t_basis_self_contraction}}}$}&\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/generic_u_basis_self_contraction}}}$}\end{array}\vskip-0.5pt

(3.6)

From the orthogonality of two-point graphs under bubble-deletion (
2.33
), and the orthogonality of the individual Clebsch-Gordan coefficients, it is easy to conclude that

\langle\mathcal{B}_{\hskip 1.5pt\mathbf{{b}}}^{\hskip 0.0pt\rho\sigma}|\mathcal{B}^{\hskip 2.2pt\mathbf{{a}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\mu\nu}\rangle\propto\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{a}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{a}}}\smash{\mathbf{b}}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mu}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mu}}\smash{\rho}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\nu}}_{\phantom{\smash{\nu}}\smash{\sigma}}\,,\vskip-0.5pt

(3.7)

and similarly for the bases
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
. To be clear: the constants of proportionality above can (and generally do) depend on the irreducible representations and multiplicity indices.

As each basis is self-orthogonal, one can determine the coefficients of expansion from one to another by computing the complete contractions such as

\hskip-20.0pt\langle\mathcal{C}_{\hskip 1.0pt\mathbf{{b}}}^{\hskip-1.0pt\rho\sigma}|\mathcal{B}^{\hskip 2.2pt\mathbf{{a}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\mu\nu}\rangle\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\;\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/ts_overlap_diagram}}}$}\quad\langle\mathcal{D}_{\hskip 2.2pt\mathbf{{c}}}^{\hskip-1.0pt\tau\upsilon}|\mathcal{B}^{\hskip 2.2pt\mathbf{{a}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\mu\nu}\rangle\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\;\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/us_overlap_diagram}}}$}\quad\langle\mathcal{D}_{\hskip 2.2pt\mathbf{{c}}}^{\hskip-1.0pt\tau\upsilon}|\mathcal{C}^{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{{b}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\rho\sigma}\rangle\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\;\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/ut_overlap_diagram}}}$}\hskip-20.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(3.8)

From these, any particular set of basis tensors can be expanded into any other; for example,
