\mathcal{B}^{\hskip 2.2pt\mathbf{{a}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\mu\nu}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b},\rho,\sigma\end{subarray}}\frac{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\hskip 1.0pt\mathbf{{b}}}^{\hskip-1.0pt\rho\sigma}|\mathcal{B}^{\hskip 2.2pt\mathbf{{a}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\mu\nu}\rangle}{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\hskip 2.2pt\mathbf{{b}}}^{\hskip-1.0pt\rho\sigma}|\mathcal{C}^{\hskip 1.2pt\mathbf{{b}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\rho\sigma}\rangle}\mathcal{C}^{\hskip 1.2pt\mathbf{{b}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\rho\sigma}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c},\tau,\upsilon\end{subarray}}\frac{\langle\mathcal{D}_{\hskip 2.2pt\mathbf{{c}}}^{\hskip-1.0pt\tau\upsilon}|\mathcal{B}^{\hskip 2.2pt\mathbf{{a}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\mu\nu}\rangle}{\langle\mathcal{D}_{\hskip 2.2pt\mathbf{{c}}}^{\hskip 0.0pt\tau\upsilon}|\mathcal{D}^{\hskip 1.2pt\mathbf{{c}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\tau\upsilon}\rangle}\mathcal{D}^{\hskip 1.2pt\mathbf{{c}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\tau\upsilon}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(3.9)

It is worth mentioning that, beyond the application of these graphs to the expansion coefficients of one basis into another, the ‘vacuum’ graph

\mathscr{F}^{\rho\sigma}_{\,\mu\nu}\big(\mathbf{q}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{R}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{S}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{T}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{U}}\,\mathbf{v}\big)\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\raisebox{8.5pt}{\text{`}}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{$\,$}c@{$\,$}c@{}}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}&\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}&\mathbf{q}\\
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}&\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}}&\mathbf{v}\end{array}\right)\raisebox{-6.0pt}{${\hskip-2.0pt}{}^{\rho\sigma}_{\mu\nu}$}\raisebox{8.5pt}{\text{'}}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/sixJ_plus_graph}}}$}\vskip-0.5pt

(3.10)

can be defined with any choice of six (irreducible) representations and for any possible values of the discrete multiplicity indices

\mu\!\in\!\big[m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}}}\smash{\mathbf{q}}}\big],\quad\nu\!\in\!\big[m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\overline{\mathbf{T}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{\mathbf{U}}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\overline{\mathbf{T}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{\mathbf{U}}}}}\smash{\overline{\mathbf{q}}}}\big],\quad\rho\!\in\!\big[m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{\mathbf{R}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{U}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{\mathbf{R}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{U}}}}\smash{\overline{\mathbf{v}}}}\big],\quad\sigma\!\in\!\big[m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\overline{\mathbf{S}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{T}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\overline{\mathbf{S}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{T}}}}\smash{\mathbf{v}}}\big]\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(3.11)

In the case of
\mathfrak{a}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1}}\!\simeq\!\mathfrak{su}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2}}
gauge theory, for which all representations are real and
m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{s}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{s}}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{t}}}}\!=\!1
for all irreducible representations, which are uniquely labeled by a single Dynkin (highest-)weight
w\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, these are related to what are called ‘six-
J
coefficients’.

3.1.1
Proof of (Non-Perturbative) Completeness

From the discussion in
[
45
]
, the number of independent tensors for any process involving external representations
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}},\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}},\ldots,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}},\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}}
is computed by the multiplicity of the irreducible representation
\mathbf{1}
in the decomposition of the tensor product representation

(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}\!\otimes\!\cdots\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}})\simeq\mathbf{1}^{m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}}}}\smash{\mathbf{1}}}}\bigoplus\ldots\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(3.12)

As the tensor-product representation
(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}\!\otimes\!\cdots\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}})
may be constructed associatively by any sequence of pairings—e.g.

(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}\!\otimes\!\cdots\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}})\simeq(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}})\!\otimes(\cdots\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}})\vskip-0.5pt

(3.13)

and any pairing may be separately decomposed into irreducible representations—e.g., taking

(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}})\simeq\bigoplus_{\text{irreps\,}\mathbf{q}}\mathbf{q}^{m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}}}\smash{\mathbf{q}}}}\vskip-0.5pt

(3.14)

we have that

(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}\!\otimes\!\cdots\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}})\simeq\bigoplus_{\text{irreps\,}\mathbf{q}}(m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}}}\smash{\mathbf{q}}})\mathbf{q}\otimes(\cdots\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}})\vskip-0.5pt

(3.15)

and we may write

m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}}}}\smash{\mathbf{1}}}=\sum_{\text{irreps}~\mathbf{q}}m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}}}\smash{\mathbf{q}}}m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{q}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{q}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}}}}\smash{\mathbf{1}}}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(3.16)

Continuing in this manner by choosing any pair of representations from those remaining will result in a formula for the total number of tensors given by a sum over multiplicities involving only three representations.

Any particular sequence of choices of representations to successively pair together can easily be seen as encoding a particular trivalent tree. For
n
external representations, these graphs involve
(n{-}2)
vertices and
(n{-}3)
nested sums over ‘internal’, irreducible representations. Interpreting these graphs as encoding particular
tensors
labeled by the irreducible representations appearing in internal edges and the multiplicity indices for each set of representations, it is clear that these form a complete basis of colour tensors.

3.1.2
Proof of Colour-Orthogonality

We saw above that the three possible bases of colour tensors for four external representations constructed via Clebsch-Gordan tensors were individually self-orthogonal (
3.6
). We would like to show that this property holds for a basis of colour tensors constructed from any
fixed
tree of Clebsch-Gordan tensors. The proof is fairly straight-forward, following inductively on the number of external legs from the orthogonality of the individual Clebsch-Gordan tensors involving three representations

\langle\overline{C}^{\nu}\!({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{\mathbf{{R^{\prime}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\overline{\mathbf{{S^{\prime}}}}}|{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\overline{\mathbf{{T^{\prime}}}}})|C_{\mu}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{R}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{S}}|{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{T}})\rangle\propto\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\nu}}_{\phantom{\smash{\nu}}\smash{\mu}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{S}^{\prime}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{S}^{\prime}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{T}^{\prime}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{T}^{\prime}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}}}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(3.17)

(To be clear: orthogonality in
\mu,\nu
indices is
not
automatic, but we may assume that all three-particle Clebsch tensors have been
chosen
or
constructed
so that this orthogonality is satisfied. This is always possible; and although potentially computationally cumbersome in general, this is not a problem for cases of greatest significance to physics—where most three-particle Clebsch-Gordan tensors are unique or easily orthogonalized via symmetrization/anti-symmetrization of their arguments.)

For any fixed trivalent tree
\Gamma
involving
n
external legs, the particular colour tensors forming the Clebsch-Gordan basis are labelled by
(n{-}3)
irreducible representations appearing on internal edges, and
(n{-}2)
multiplicity indices (representing the span of tensors at each vertex). That is, considering the set of tensors

\mathcal{B}(\Gamma)\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\big\{\mathcal{B}_{\mu\cdots\sigma}^{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}\big\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\text{irreps}\,\mathbf{a},\ldots,\mathbf{c}$}}\\
\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\text{multiplicities }\mu,\ldots,\sigma$}}\end{subarray}}\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\hskip 80.0pt,$}}\vskip-6.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(3.18)

we need to show that

\langle\mathcal{B}^{\smash{\mu^{\prime}\!\cdots\sigma^{\prime}}}_{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a^{\prime}}\!\cdots\mathbf{c^{\prime}}}|\mathcal{B}_{\mu\cdots\sigma}^{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}\rangle\propto\big(\delta^{\mu^{\prime}}_{\mu}\cdots\delta^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{\sigma}\big)\big(\delta^{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathbf{a^{\prime}}}\cdots\delta^{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathbf{c^{\prime}}}\big)\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(3.19)

Suppose for the sake of induction that colour-orthogonality is true for all fixed graphs involving
n\!\geq\!3
external representations (This, we reiterate, is a choice one can freely make for the trivalent Clebsch-Gordan coefficients.) We must show that orthogonality is implied for any graph involving
(n{+}1)
external representations. Let
\widehat{\Gamma}
represent any fixed trivalent tree whose legs are labelled by
(n{+}1)
external representations. Any such graph must be of the form

\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip-4.0pt:\hskip 1.25pt}$}}\hskip-150.0pt

(3.20)

for
some
pair of external edges (which we have, without loss of generality, taken to encode ‘outgoing’ representations ‘
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}
’ and ‘
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}
’). That is, as a tensor,

\mathcal{B}({\widehat{\Gamma}})\ni\widehat{C}^{\smash{\,\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}q}}}}_{\,\cdots\mu}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip-4.0pt:\hskip 1.25pt}$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}q}\in{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[q]}}C_{\Gamma}(\cdots|\cdots\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}q}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\cdots}}_{\phantom{\smash{\cdots}}\smash{\cdots\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}q}}}C_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}q}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}q}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}q}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]}}}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(3.21)

From this, we can easily see that the orthogonality of Clebsch-Gordan tensors involving
n\!\geq\!3
external legs ensures complete orthogonality.

3.2
Comparing Bases Built from Different Trees: Duality & Crossing

From the discussions above, it is clear that there are in fact
many
possible colour-orthogonal bases to choose for any process involving arbitrary representations: not only must one choose some representative trivalent tree, but also the ordering of the representations among the graph’s external edges. Each choice results in a distinct, self-orthogonal basis of colour tensors which span the space of all possible colour tensors—including those which may not arise via perturbation theory.

As illustrated in our discussion of four-particle Clebsch bases around equation (
3.5
) above, the expansion of any particular basis tensor into any other basis follows from the general decomposition of any colour tensor whatsoever. As discussed in
section
1.2
, orthogonality of any choice of basis ensures that colour tensors may be decomposed via

C({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{R}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{S}}\cdots\!|\!\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{T}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{U}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}\\
\mu\cdots\sigma\end{subarray}}\frac{\langle\mathcal{B}^{\smash{\mu\cdots\sigma}}_{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}|C\rangle}{\langle\mathcal{B}^{\smash{\mu\cdots\sigma}}_{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}|\mathcal{B}_{\mu\cdots\sigma}^{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}\rangle}\mathcal{B}_{\mu\cdots\sigma}^{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{R}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{S}}\cdots\!|\!\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{T}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{U}})\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(3.22)

Thus, the colour basis given above in (
3.5
) represented by colour tensors
\{\mathcal{B}_{\mu\cdots\sigma}^{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}\}
of the basis generated by any particular graph may be expressed in terms of the colour tensors of the basis represented by some other choice of graph
\{\mathcal{C}_{\hskip 2.0pt\tau\cdots\upsilon}^{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{d}\cdots\mathbf{f}}\}
via

\mathcal{B}_{\mu\cdots\sigma}^{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\\[-2.0pt]
\smash{\mathbf{d},\ldots\mathbf{f}}\\
\smash{\tau,\ldots,\upsilon}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{M}^{\smash{\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c},\hskip 1.0pt\tau\cdots\upsilon}}_{\smash{\mu\cdots\rho,\hskip 2.0pt\mathbf{d}\cdots\mathbf{f}}}\,\,\mathcal{C}_{\hskip 2.0pt\tau\cdots\upsilon}^{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{d}\cdots\mathbf{f}}\vskip-0.5pt

(3.23)

where the coefficients of the expansion are given by

\mathbf{M}^{\smash{\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c},\hskip 1.0pt\tau\cdots\upsilon}}_{\smash{\mu\cdots\rho,\hskip 2.0pt\mathbf{d}\cdots\mathbf{f}}}\,\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\frac{\langle\mathcal{C}^{\hskip 1.0pt\tau\cdots\upsilon}_{\hskip 1.5pt\mathbf{d}\cdots\mathbf{f}}|\mathcal{B}_{\mu\cdots\sigma}^{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}\rangle}{\langle\mathcal{C}^{\hskip 1.0pt\tau\cdots\upsilon}_{\hskip 1.5pt\mathbf{d}\cdots\mathbf{f}}|\mathcal{C}_{\hskip 2.0pt\tau\cdots\upsilon}^{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{d}\cdots\mathbf{f}}\rangle}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(3.24)

We call these numbers ‘
duality
coefficients’ as they relate one basis to another. As in other examples we have seen, these numbers are computed in terms of ‘vacuum graphs’ of Clebsch tensors—those with no external representation indices.

Note that the duality coefficients
\mathbf{M}
relate bases defined with identical cyclic orderings of the external legs. It turns out that all these changes of bases can be generated via sequences of those arising from four or fewer legs; in particular

\mathscr{F}^{\smash{\text{\makebox[9.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\mathbf{a}\hskip-150.0pt$}},\text{\makebox[9.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\rho\sigma\hskip-150.0pt$}}}}_{\smash{\text{\makebox[9.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\mu\nu\hskip-150.0pt$}},\text{\makebox[9.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\mathbf{b}\hskip-150.0pt$}}}}\!\!:\!\!\!\left(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/four_point_generic_basis_s.pdf}}}$}\right)\!\!\mapsto\!\!\left\{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/four_point_generic_basis_t.pdf}}}$}\right\}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(3.25)

Combined with the twist operation (
3.3
)
\mathscr{R}
, we can connect all possible graphs to one another. Moreover, this can be done in multiple distinct ways—for example:

The above sequences of maps can be understood as the identity that

\mathscr{F}\!\circ\!\mathscr{R}\!\circ\!\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{R}\!\circ\!\mathscr{F}\!\circ\!\mathscr{R}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(3.26)

For larger graphs, it is easy to see that sequences of four-leg
\mathscr{F}
operations acting on single edges of the graph can connect any two plane graphs with consistent orderings of the external labels; for five particles, for example, we could connect all graphs via:

(3.27)

This, when spelled out in terms of specific tensors, corresponds to the identity:

\mathscr{F}^{\smash{\hskip 2.5pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{r}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{s}}\hskip 2.5pt,\upsilon\omega\tau}}_{\hskip 1.2pt\smash{\mu\nu\hskip-1.0pt\rho,\hskip 2.5pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{p}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{t}}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}r}},\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}t}}\\
\overline{\mu},\sigma,\tau\end{subarray}}\hskip-2.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathscr{F}}^{\smash{\hskip 2.5pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{r}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{s}}\hskip 2.5pt,\overline{\mu}\sigma\tau}}_{\smash{\mu\nu\hskip-1.0pt\rho,\hskip 2.5pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{r}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{t}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathscr{F}}^{\smash{\hskip 3.5pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{r}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{t}}\hskip 3.5pt,\upsilon\omega\tau}}_{\hskip 1.2pt\smash{\overline{\mu}\sigma\hskip 0.0pt\tau,\hskip 2.5pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{p}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{t}}}}=\hskip-10.0pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}q}},\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.4431,0.2862,0.796}s}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{q}},{\color[rgb]{0.4431,0.2862,0.796}\mathbf{p}}\\
\eta,\theta,\overline{\rho},\overline{\eta},\epsilon,\upsilon\end{subarray}}\hskip-10.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathscr{F}}^{\smash{\hskip 2.5pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{r}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{s}}\hskip 2.5pt,\eta\theta\overline{\rho}}}_{\hskip 1.2pt\smash{\mu\nu\hskip-1.0pt\rho,\hskip 2.5pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{q}}{\color[rgb]{0.4431,0.2862,0.796}{\mathbf{s}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4431,0.2862,0.796}\mathscr{F}}^{\smash{\hskip 2.5pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{q}}{\color[rgb]{0.4431,0.2862,0.796}\mathbf{s}}\hskip 2.5pt,\overline{\eta}\epsilon\upsilon}}_{\hskip 1.2pt\smash{\hskip 0.75pt\eta\theta\hskip-0.5pt\overline{\rho},\hskip 2.5pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{q}}{\color[rgb]{0.4431,0.2862,0.796}{\mathbf{p}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathscr{F}}^{\smash{\hskip 2.5pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{q}}{\color[rgb]{0.4431,0.2862,0.796}\mathbf{p}}\hskip 1.05pt,\upsilon\omega\tau}}_{\hskip 1.2pt\smash{\hskip 0.75pt\overline{\eta}\epsilon\upsilon,\hskip 2.5pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{p}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}{\mathbf{t}}}}}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(3.28)

It is a well-known folk theorem that combining sequences of
\mathscr{F}
operations with three-leg
\mathscr{R}
operators acting on single vertices, any pair of plane graphs with arbitrary orderings of external edge labels (but the same number of edges and vertices) can be mapped to one another.

Identities such as (
3.26
) and (
3.28
) are guaranteed to hold as a consequence of the completeness of the bases of tensors represented by each; but it is interesting to take a more ‘categorical’ view of the situation.

We may instead view the
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{R}
as general maps between abstract objects; and view the commutativity relations of (
3.26
) or (
3.28
) as
constraints
which these maps must satisfy. The existence of Clebsch-Gordan colour tensor bases ensures that this category is non-trivially realizable. In this context, relations (
3.26
) and (
3.28
) are known as the hexagon and petagon relations, and are part of the
coherrence conditions
of the category. The Biedenharn-Elliott relation among six-
J
symbols and the famous 5-term Dilog identity are both instantiations of the categorical pentagon relation: the unification of seemingly unrelated relations is a hallmark of ‘categorification’. For higher-point trees one can construct similar webs of interrelations, but these turn out to generate no new constraints. For more on monoidal tensor categories we direct the reader to standard references such as
[
60
,
61
,
62
,
63
]
.

This attitude toward the duality maps is analogous to viewing Plücker relations for the Grassmannian as
constraints
imposed upon abstract ‘coordinates’ labelled by
k
-tuples: when these symbols are
defined
in terms of actual determinants, then all Plücker relations are trivially satisfied as implications of Cramer’s rule; but a more abstract view is occasionally powerful. It is also intriguing that similar structures arise in scattering amplitudes through connections to cluster algebras
[
64
,
65
]
. It would be interesting to pursue such connections in the future.

3.3
Colour Tensor Decomposition and Reduction

Completeness of any Clebsch-Gordan colour tensor basis ensures that
any
tensor involving the same set of external representations can be represented with unique coefficients in that basis:

C(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}\cdots\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}U}})\in\mathrm{span}\big\{\mathcal{B}_{\mu\cdots\sigma}^{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}\big\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\text{irreps }\mathbf{a},\ldots,\mathbf{c}$}}\\
\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\text{multiplicies }\mu,\ldots,\sigma$}}\end{subarray}}\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\hskip 80.0pt,$}}\vskip-6.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(3.29)

where these basis graphs are chosen for any fixed choice of trivalent tree
\Gamma
. Moreover, orthogonality of the tensors within any such basis ensures that the coefficients of the expansion of
C
into the basis
\{\mathcal{B}\}
can be computed directly via

C({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{R}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{S}}\cdots\!|{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{T}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{U}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}\\
\mu\cdots\sigma\end{subarray}}\frac{\langle\mathcal{B}^{\smash{\mu\cdots\sigma}}_{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}|C\rangle}{\langle\mathcal{B}^{\smash{\mu\cdots\sigma}}_{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}|\mathcal{B}_{\mu\cdots\sigma}^{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}\rangle}\mathcal{B}_{\mu\cdots\sigma}^{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{R}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\mathbf{S}}|{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}\mathbf{T}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{U}})\,\vskip-0.5pt

(3.30)

where the non-trivial computation of

\langle\mathcal{B}^{\smash{\mu\cdots\sigma}}_{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}|C\rangle\vskip-0.5pt

(3.31)

can always be considered as a vacuum graph connecting the (conjugated) tree
\Gamma
to a graph encoding the colour tensor
C
.

While the statements above are quite general and powerful, it is useful to observe that these coefficients can also be determined by iterated sequences of smaller duality relations which
decompose
a given graph into those of any chosen basis.

3.3.1
Direct
Decomposition
of Arbitrary Colour Tensors

Suppose one is given some arbitrary colour tensor defined by some arbitrary graph. We would like to show how the operations
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{R}
(viewed as local maps acting on single edges and vertices) can be used to systematically decompose any graph into those of any chosen basis.

Actually, these two operations alone cannot connect
arbitrary
graphs, as neither
\mathscr{F}
nor
\mathscr{R}
affects the Euler characteristic of a graph. We need one further operation:
bubble-deletion
defined

\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}[s]},{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}t}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}[t]}}\mathbf{\overline{C}}^{\nu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}u}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}t}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}t}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[u]}}}\mathbf{C}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}t}}}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip-4.0pt:\hskip 1.25pt}$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,\mathscr{B}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}})\,\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[u]}}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\nu}}_{\phantom{\smash{\nu}}\smash{\mu}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}u}}}}\vskip-0.5pt

(3.32)

which we can represent graphically as the statement that

\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/bubble_deletion_general_1}}}$}\hskip 5.0pt\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip-4.0pt:\hskip 1.25pt}$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\;\mathscr{B}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}S}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}T}})\;\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/identity_r_v2}}}$}\vskip-0.5pt

(3.33)

which we can understand as an instance of the more general implication of Schur’s lemma, (
2.33
)—holding for any pair of irreducible representations
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}
and
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}u}}
. It is worth noting that these coefficients are related to the
\Theta
coefficients defined in (
2.74
) many ways:

\begin{split}\Theta_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}t}})&=\mathscr{B}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}t}})\,\,\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}})\\
&=\sum_{\nu\in[m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}t}}}}]}\mathscr{R}_{\mu}^{\phantom{\mu}\,\nu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}})\mathscr{B}_{\nu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}t}})\,\,\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}s}})\\
&=\mathscr{B}_{\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}t}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}\overline{s}}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}})\,\,\mathrm{dim}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}t}})\end{split}\vskip-10.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(3.34)

and so-on.

Combined with the operations
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{R}
, any colour tensor graph can be reduced into any chosen basis. The proof is semi-trivial: we have already seen how sequences of
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{R}
can be used to connect any pair of tree-level tensor diagrams, allowing one to ‘expand’ any colour tensor encoded by a tree into a basis specified by some other fixed tree; therefore, we must only see that colour tensors encoded by graphs of higher Euler-characteristic can be reduced to trees.

The argument is very simple. Consider an arbitrary tensor diagram of
girth

g
. Recall that the girth of a graph is the size of its smallest cycle. If a graph has girth 2, then use bubble-deletion to reduce it to trees; if its girth is greater than 2, then pick any edge along a minimal-length cycle, and apply the
\mathscr{F}
operation (an ‘st-duality’); the resulting graph will be of strictly lower girth; repeat until there exists a 2-cycle, and then apply bubble deletion. To illustrate this point, consider the reduction of a girth-4 graph via:

\hskip-240.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/box_to_tree_sequence_0}}}$}\hskip-4.5pt\overset{\mathscr{F}}{\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\longrightarrow$}}}\hskip-4.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/box_to_tree_sequence_1}}}$}\hskip-4.5pt\overset{\mathscr{F}}{\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\longrightarrow$}}}\hskip-4.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/box_to_tree_sequence_2}}}$}\hskip-4.5pt\overset{\mathscr{B}}{\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\longrightarrow$}}}\hskip-4.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/box_to_tree_sequence_3}}}$}\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$.$}}\hskip-220.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(3.35)

It is clear that by repeating this process, any graph can be systematically decomposed into those with vanishing Euler characteristic—namely, trees. Then, it is a trivial fact that any tree can be connected to any other via sequences of
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{R}
operations, thereby expanding the original graph into those of any chosen basis.

It is also clear that there is no unique way to do this decomposition, and a different reduction sequence can land on the same tree at the end. The equivalence of these reductions implies relations between the coefficient six-
J
s, the most famous being the Biedenharn-Elliott identity
[
66
]
.

3.3.2
Computing Coefficients via Duality Sequences

It is worthwhile to see how this works in practice. Suppose that we are given a colour tensor generated by some multi-loop Feynman diagram. The colour tensors arising via Feynman rules always involve tensors arising via graphs of representation matrices and structure constants—both of which can be understood as particular instances of Clebsch-Gordan tensors, sewn together. Thus, we may apply sequences of maps (in many distinct ways) to systematically decompose the tensor into a chosen basis.

The upshot of this approach is that the requisite maps,
\mathscr{F}
,
\mathscr{R}
, and
\mathscr{B}
may be computed (and tabulated, say) once and for all for a wide array of possible arguments. Recall that these maps depend on various irreducible representations and multiplicity indices, which in principle span unbounded (although countable) ranges of possible arguments; but the ranges of arguments required for the decomposition of a given class of tensors—those involving external adjoint-charged particles, say—can be manageably limited in scope.

More directly, we have seen that the coefficients of an arbitrary colour tensor
C
into any colour-diagonal basis can be computed in terms of vacuum diagrams representing the complete contractions representing
\langle\mathcal{B}^{\smash{\mu\cdots\sigma}}_{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{a}\cdots\mathbf{c}}|C\rangle
. These vacuum graphs are clearly reducible to ‘empty’ trees via the same sequences of moves as described above. For example, the sequence of moves (
3.35
) can be applied to any
subgraph
—it not being necessary that the edges corresponding to the representations
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}},\ldots,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}u}}
in (
3.35
), for example, be ‘external’. This procedure reduces all vacuum graphs to sums of products of six-
J
coefficients, which can be further reduced to a
minimal
set of independent six-
J
coefficients to be calculated once and for all, as has been discussed in
e.g.

[
67
]
and
[
68
]
. Such symbolic approaches can be extremely powerful for dealing with tensors associated to large graphs or ones dressed with high-dimensional representations, settings where explicit tensor contractions may be prohibitively costly in comparison.

4
Comparisons with Other Choices for Colour Tensor ‘Bases’

Although Clebsch-Gordan coefficients are well known to physicists, the bases of colour tensors described here are several steps removed from those which appear directly in the Feynman rules of gauge theory or those frequently encountered in the study of scattering amplitudes among coloured particles. As described in
section
1.1
, the Feynman rules of gauge theory (
1.1
)
directly
make reference to the generators of various representations of Lie algebras. As we have seen in
section
2.3
, these representations, encoded by rank-three tensors appearing directly in the Lagrangian defining the gauge theory, can in fact be understood as particular Clebsch-Gordan tensors:

\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip-4.0pt:\hskip 1.25pt}$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,C_{1}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(4.1)

But the
particular
colour tensor involved in any one Feynman diagram is of course fairly meaningless: these tensors satisfy many linear relations arising from both general aspects of Lie algebras (such as the Jacobi identity) and many peculiar relations specific to particular representations of particular algebras. Such relations are necessitated by the gauge
non-invariance
of individual Feynman diagrams diagrams.

Due to this redundancy, a number of familiar collections of convenient colour tensors have been used to represent amplitudes with various degrees of completeness, redundancy, and generality, but almost universally suffering from colour non-orthogonality.

One salient feature of most of these frameworks is that they often involve tensors defined directly (or closely) in terms of the generators defining the representations of coloured particles. This endows them with a sense of naturalness that may appear absent from the Clebsch colour tensor bases described above in
section
3
. These new tensors, by contrast, are defined in terms of irreducible representations often
not
corresponding to those of the coloured particles involved in a process (or even present in the theory).

Consider for example the case of
\mathfrak{so}_{10}
gauge theory with a single particle charged in the fundamental
(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}10}}
-dimensional) representation ‘
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}F}}
’. For a two-to-two process involving this particle and an adjoint-charged gluon, we would need colour tensors of the form
C(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}F}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}F}})
. There are three linearly independent tensors for this process (to arbitrary orders of perturbation theory). As we will see below in
section
4.3.2
, one choice for a Clebsch colour basis of tensors would consist of

(\mathfrak{so}_{{10}})
\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\left\{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/so10_clebsch_basis_1}}}$},\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/so10_clebsch_basis_2}}}$},\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/so10_clebsch_basis_3}}}$}\right\}\,.\hskip-150.0pt

(4.2)

While the first tensor above is obviously ‘natural’, one may naturally wonder: what on earth do the
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}120}}
-dimensional or
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}320}}
-dimensional irreducible representations of
\mathfrak{so}_{10}
have to do with this process?—and why should these tensors be useful?

In answer to the first question, it is worth noting that the representations
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}120}}
and
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}320}}
arise directly in the tensor product

(\mathfrak{so}_{{10}})
\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 12.9863pt\hskip-3.61803pt\hbox{$\text{\rule[-5.0pt]{0.0pt}{10.0pt}{\normalsize$\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}F}}$}}$}\hskip-3.61803pt\hskip-12.9863pt\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox{$\rule[-4.0pt]{0.0pt}{10.0pt}\text{{\footnotesize$[10000]$}}$}}\hskip-12.9863pt\hskip 12.9863pt}\!\otimes\!\hbox{\hskip 12.9863pt\hskip-5.98953pt\hbox{$\text{\rule[-5.0pt]{0.0pt}{10.0pt}{\normalsize$\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}$}}$}\hskip-5.98953pt\hskip-12.9863pt\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox{$\rule[-4.0pt]{0.0pt}{10.0pt}\text{{\footnotesize$[01000]$}}$}}\hskip-12.9863pt\hskip 12.9863pt}\simeq\hbox{\hskip 12.9863pt\hskip-3.61803pt\hbox{$\text{\rule[-5.0pt]{0.0pt}{10.0pt}{\normalsize$\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}F}}$}}$}\hskip-3.61803pt\hskip-12.9863pt\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox{$\rule[-4.0pt]{0.0pt}{10.0pt}\text{{\footnotesize$[10000]$}}$}}\hskip-12.9863pt\hskip 12.9863pt}\!\oplus\!\hbox{\hskip 12.9863pt\hskip-8.62495pt\hbox{$\text{\rule[-5.0pt]{0.0pt}{10.0pt}{\normalsize$\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}120}}$}}$}\hskip-8.62495pt\hskip-12.9863pt\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox{$\rule[-4.0pt]{0.0pt}{10.0pt}\text{{\footnotesize$[00100]$}}$}}\hskip-12.9863pt\hskip 12.9863pt}\!\oplus\!\hbox{\hskip 12.9863pt\hskip-8.62495pt\hbox{$\text{\rule[-5.0pt]{0.0pt}{10.0pt}{\normalsize$\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.545,0.7451}320}}$}}$}\hskip-8.62495pt\hskip-12.9863pt\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\hbox{$\rule[-4.0pt]{0.0pt}{10.0pt}\text{{\footnotesize$[11000]$}}$}}\hskip-12.9863pt\hskip 12.9863pt}\,.\hskip-150.0pt

(4.3)

As such, it is not so surprising that these irreducible representations should play a role—it is what one may expect from studying scattering in elementary quantum mechanics.

As for their usefulness, we remind the reader that they are manifestly colour-orthogonal, which would not be the case for the more arguably ‘natural’ tensors, say:

(\mathfrak{so}_{{10}})
\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\left\{\mathbf{S}^{1},\mathbf{S}^{2},\mathbf{S}^{3}\right\}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\left\{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/so10_s_basis_1}}}$},\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/so10_s_basis_2}}}$},\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/so10_s_basis_3}}}$}\right\}\,.\hskip-150.0pt

(4.4)

These tensors are in fact densely overlapping in colour space:

\langle\mathbf{S}_{i}|\mathbf{S}^{j}\rangle=\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}ccc@{}}\frac{405}{2}&\frac{45}{2}&45\\
\frac{45}{2}&\frac{405}{2}&45\\
45&45&450\end{array}\right)\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(4.5)

One could view the novel tensors in the Clebsch basis as being defined in terms of these more natural ones via

\displaystyle\hskip-2.0pt=\frac{1}{3}\left(\!2\!\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/so10_s_basis_2}}}$}-\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/so10_s_basis_3}}}$}\right)

(4.6)

\displaystyle\hskip-2.0pt=\frac{1}{3}\!\left(-\frac{1}{3}\!\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/so10_s_basis_1}}}$}\hskip-2.0pt+\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/so10_s_basis_2}}}$}\hskip-2.0pt+\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/so10_s_basis_3}}}$}\right)\hskip-200.0pt

and take these unusual tensors to be a simple change of basis that happens to realize orthogonality in colour space. While this is a valid viewpoint, it is by no means obvious how to generalize this case to more general scattering amplitudes involving more (or more diversely) coloured particles. For one thing, even when a set of ‘natural’ colour tensors such as (
4.4
) exist, their completeness or their linear independence can be far from obvious.

In this section, we’d like to discuss more examples analogous to the case above at greater length. In particular, we’d like to explore how these new tensors relate to the more familiar colour tensors that have often been used. But in order to do this, a brief review of those more familiar tensors may be useful.

4.1
Familiar Sets of Colour Tensors for Representing Amplitudes

4.1.1
Feynman Diagrams for Adjoint Scattering: ‘
f
-Graphs’

For amplitudes involving particles only charged in the adjoint representation of the gauge group’s Lie algebra, the only tensor which appears directly in the Feynman rules will be
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}
itself. Recall that this representation is identical (as a tensor) to what are often called the
structure
constants of the Lie algebra
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]\,[\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]\,[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}
. (The gauge-kinetic term may be chosen to involve a four-particle interaction vertex, but the colour dependence of this term is directly given as a sum of products of
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
’s.)

Thus, the colour tensors that arise directly from the Feynman rules are those representable as graphs of structure constants sewn together. These tensors are called ‘
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
-graphs’. At
L
loops, these tensors will be encoded by graphs with Euler characteristic
L
. It has become fairly standard practice to express amplitudes involving adjoint-charged particles directly in terms of such tensors (see, for example, the works of
[
69
,
29
,
41
,
70
,
44
,
71
,
21
]
), viewed abstractly (or implicitly) as encoding tensors as graphs built from structure constants, with the understanding that the reader can use whatever concrete structure constants she may choose to realize these terms concretely. Expressing amplitudes in this way has the advantage that expressions remain equally valid for any particular gauge theory. Another advantage is that the tensors relevant to amplitudes arise
perturbatively
in loop-order, with only a restricted subset of all possible tensors arising.

Of course,
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
-graphs are not always linearly independent: minimally, they satisfy the Jacobi relation. This implies that not all
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
-graphs are independent at any loop-order. Moreover, identities such as

\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/triangle_for_adjoint_id_0}}}$}=\frac{1}{2}C_{2}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}})\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/triangle_for_adjoint_id_1}}}$}\,\vskip-6.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(4.7)

and also those peculiar to particular Lie algebras allow
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
-graphs at distinct loop-orders to be linearly related to one another.

It is a general folk theorem (not entirely difficult to prove) that all tree-level
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
-graphs can be related to one another via simple applications of the Jacobi relation; in particular, a ‘basis’ of independent
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
-graphs can be chosen of the form

{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}a_{2}\cdots a_{\text{-}2}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{\text{-}1}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}a_{2}\cdots a_{\text{-}2}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{\text{-}1}}}}\smash{}}\;\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/ddm_basis_tensor_structure}}}$}\vskip-6.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(4.8)

together with the
(n{-}2)!
permutations in the slot-sequences of the blue indices (keeping the first and last slots fixed). Moreover, for any choice of initial and final legs
\{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a},{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}c}\}\!\subset\![n]
, the set of tensors obtained by permuting the remaining
(n{-}2)!
other slot sequences
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\vec{b}}\!\in\!\mathfrak{S}([n]\backslash\{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a},{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}c}\})
will form a ‘basis’ of tensors independent under Jacobi relations.

This basis of colour tensors is most familiar from the work of DDM
[
47
]
. The equality of amplitudes represented according to different choices of tree-level ‘bases’ (distinguished by the choice of initial and final legs
\{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a},{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}c}\}\!\subset\![n]
held fixed) requires that the partial amplitudes satisfy the Kleiss-Kuijf-relations
[
72
]
which generalize the famous
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
decoupling identity
[
73
,
74
]
(see also
[
40
]
).

We have used scare-quotes around ‘basis’ above for two reasons. First, the number of such tensors increases factorially with multiplicity, whereas the number of independent colour tensors to
arbitrary
loop-order is bounded to grow exponentially
[
45
]
; thus, for any simple Lie algebra, there will be a multiplicity
n
for which not all
tree-level

{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
-graphs will be linearly independent. The second reason for having caution against declaring these tensors a
basis
is that in almost all cases, these tensors are
incomplete
: new, linearly-independent
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
-graphs arise at higher loop-orders.

It is worthwhile to mention a few cases where these issues are most transparent. For the case of
\mathfrak{a}_{1}
gauge theory, the tree-level
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
-graph tensors are
over
-complete for
n\!\geq\!6
external legs, and
incomplete
for any even number of particles. (Interestingly, for all even multiplicities, only one independent tensor exists beyond tree-level.) For
n{=}5
particles and gauge theories with rank exceeding 1, all
6{=}(5{-}2)!
tree-level
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
-graphs are independent; but new, linearly independent tensors arise at both one and two loops. (For most simple Lie algebras, no new tensors arise beyond two loops for 5 particles; but for
\mathfrak{a}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}>1}
, these tensors are still
incomplete
3
3

3

If one considered both
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
-graphs and ‘
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}d}
-graphs’—those built from the
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}d}
tensor defined in (
2.56
)—then graphs with Euler characteristic
\leq\!2
would be complete for 5 particles; interestingly, however, because
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{ad}}
is necessarily
real
, it is not possible to define the
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}d}
tensor in terms of
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
alone. This coheres with our understanding that
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}d}
tensor contributions to amplitudes signal
anomalies
in the gauge theory, which can only arise from matter transforming in complex representations.
.)

More pervasively, these tensors are highly non-orthogonal in colour space. For example, for
\mathfrak{a}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1}}
gauge theory, the
24{=}(6{-}2)!
tree-level DDM tensors have overlap:

\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\frac{\langle{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\vec{a}})|{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\vec{b}})\rangle}{\langle{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\vec{a}})|{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\vec{a}})\rangle}=\frac{1}{8}\!$}}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{$\,\,$}c@{}}8&4&4&2&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&4&2&2&1&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&4&3&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&3&3\\[-6.0pt]
4&8&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&4&2&2&4&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&1&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&3&3&2&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&4&3\\[-6.0pt]
4&2&8&4&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&2&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&4&3&4&2&2&1&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&3&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3\\[-6.0pt]
2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&4&8&2&4&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&3&3&2&4&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&1&1&2&4&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}\\[-6.0pt]
2&4&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&8&4&1&2&4&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&3&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&4&2&2&1&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}\\[-6.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&2&4&4&8&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&3&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&1&2&4&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&4&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&1\\[-6.0pt]
4&2&2&1&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&8&4&4&2&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&1&3&4&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&3\\[-6.0pt]
2&4&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&1&4&8&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&4&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&1&3&4\\[-6.0pt]
2&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&4&3&4&2&8&4&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&2&1&4&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&3&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}\\[-6.0pt]
1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&3&3&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&4&8&2&4&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&4&1&2&4&3&1&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}\\[-6.0pt]
1&2&4&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&4&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&8&4&3&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&1&4&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1\\[-6.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&3&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&2&4&4&8&4&3&1&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&4&1&2\\[-6.0pt]
2&1&4&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&1&3&4&8&4&4&2&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&3&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}\\[-6.0pt]
1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&4&1&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&3&4&8&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&4&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&4&2&1\\[-6.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&1&3&4&2&1&4&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&4&2&8&4&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&3&3&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1\\[-6.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&3&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&4&1&2&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&4&8&2&4&3&4&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&2\\[-6.0pt]
4&3&1&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&4&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&8&4&1&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&4&2\\[-6.0pt]
3&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&4&3&1&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&2&4&4&8&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&1&2&2&4\\[-6.0pt]
1&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&4&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&4&2&1&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&3&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&8&4&4&2&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}\\[-6.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&1&2&2&4&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&3&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&4&2&1&4&8&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&4&2\\[-6.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&4&2&1&1&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&4&2&3&3&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&4&2&8&4&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2\\[-6.0pt]
3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&3&3&1&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&1&2&2&4&3&4&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&2&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&4&8&2&4\\[-6.0pt]
3&4&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&2&3&3&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&1&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&4&2&2&4&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&8&4\\[-6.0pt]
3&3&3&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&3&4&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&2&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&1&1&2&2&4&{\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}0}&2&2&4&4&8\end{array}\right)\vskip-12.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(4.9)

This overlap does not diminish in the large-rank limit.

4.1.2
Multi-Trace Colour Tensors and the Large-Rank Limit

Even for amplitudes not involving any charged matter (and therefore without any reference to any particular representation other than
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}
), it is often useful to use tensors constructed from
some
representation to define tensors involving
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}
. This follows from the simple fact that for
any
representation
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}
, we have

{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{}}\,=\frac{1}{T(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}})}\big(\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1\,2\,3})-\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2\,1\,3})\big)\,;\vskip-6.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(4.10)

this is equivalent to the graphical statement that

\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/triangle_for_adjoint_id_1}}}$}=\frac{1}{T(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}})}\!\left(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/triangle_for_structure_1}}}$}-\hskip-2.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/triangle_for_structure_2}}}$}\hskip-4.0pt\right).\vskip-6.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(4.11)

From this, we can directly convert any tensor defined as an
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
-graph directly into those involving traces over the generators of any particular representation.

Applying (
4.11
) directly to every vertex of an
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
-graph such as (
4.8
) may at first appear to horribly complicate things. At tree-level, however, it is easy to show recursively (using the defining commutation relation (
2.8
) for any representation) that all tree-level
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
graphs are expressible in terms of sums of
single
-traces over generators of any representation:

\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{n}})\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{i}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{R}}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{2}}}}\!\!\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\mathbf{R}}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{n}}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{n}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{n}}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{n}}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r_{1}}}}\big)\;\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/trace_over_generators_diagram}}}$}\vskip-5.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(4.12)

which is naturally upgraded to the entire rank-
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
tensor

\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\cdots}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n})\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]\cdots[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\big\{\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{n}})\big\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{i}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}\;\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/trace_over_generators_tensor}}}$}\,.\vskip-5.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(4.13)

These tensors manifestly enjoy a rotational symmetry among their indices. Reflections, however, are more subtle: reversing the arrows of the traced representation corresponds to tracing over the generators of the
conjugate
representation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{\mathbf{R}}}
:

\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{n}})=(\text{-}1)^{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{R}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{n}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}})\,\;\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/dual_of_trace_diagram_1}}}$}\hskip-10.0pt=(\text{-}1)^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\hskip-10.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/dual_of_trace_diagram_2}}}$}.\vskip-5.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(4.14)

Upgrading this statement to the level of tensors, we find it useful to define

\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\cdots}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1})\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\big\{\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{n}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}})\big\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{i}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}};\vskip-0.5pt

(4.15)

that is, we consider ‘
\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}\cdots a_{n}})
’ to denote the tensor with a
permuted
sequence of ‘slots’ dictated by the ordering of the sequence
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}
.

If
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\!\simeq\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}{R}}}}}
, then single-trace tensors with reflected orderings of arguments will be identical up to an overall sign; as such, there are generally at most
(n{-}1)!/2
possibly independent tensors. For complex representations, however, all
(n{-}1)!
tensors will be distinct in general. To be clear, these tensors
necessarily
fail to be independent beyond some multiplicity of external legs for any fixed Lie algebra as the number of independent tensors (given by
m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{ad}}^{\otimes n}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{ad}}^{\otimes n}}}\smash{\mathbf{1}}}
) can grow at most exponentially.

At tree-level, reality of the representation
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}
necessitates that only
self-conjugate
combinations of tensors appear. In particular,

\hskip-230.0pt\overline{\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{2}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{n}})}\;\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/dual_of_trace_indices_diagram_1}}}$}\hskip-15.0pt=(\text{-}1)^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\hskip-15.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/dual_of_trace_indices_diagram_2}}}$}\hskip-15.0pt=(\text{-}1)^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\hskip-15.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/dual_of_trace_indices_diagram_3}}}$}\hskip-1.0pt;\hskip-15.0pt\hskip-180.0pt\vskip-0.5pt

(4.16)

which is to say,

\overline{\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1\,2\cdots n})}=\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\overline{R}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{1}\,\overline{2}\cdots\overline{n}})=(\text{-}1)^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\overline{n}\cdots\overline{2}\,\overline{1}})\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(4.17)

Here, we may be overly pedantic to note that conjugated adjoint index means simply

C{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\overline{\mathfrak{g}}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\overline{\mathfrak{g}}]}}}\smash{}}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}\in{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}C{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{}}_{\phantom{\smash{}}\smash{\!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}}}\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\overline{\mathfrak{g}}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\overline{\mathfrak{g}}]}}}\smash{}}=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}\in{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}\big(\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}b}\in{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}C{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}b}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}b}}}\smash{}}g^{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}}_{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}b}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}}}\big)\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\overline{\mathfrak{g}}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\overline{\mathfrak{g}}]}}}\smash{}}\,,\vskip-0.5pt

(4.18)

and we generally do
not
assume that these indices have been arranged so that
g_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{ad}}}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}\!\propto\!\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{}}
.

In terms of these, we may define the
complete contraction
of the tensor
\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1\cdots n})
with its conjugate via

\begin{split}\langle\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1\,2\cdots n})|\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1\,2\cdots n})\rangle&\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{i}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}\overline{\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1\,2\cdots n})}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{}}_{\phantom{\smash{}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}}}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1\,2\cdots n}){\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}}}\smash{}}\\
&\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/self_contraction_of_trace}}}$}\,.\end{split}\vskip-0.5pt

(4.19)

According to the convention that the Killing metric be normalized so that
g_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}][\mathfrak{g}]}}}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}F}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1\,2})
, we can easily compute these for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}n}
copies of the
fundamental
representation
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}F}}
of the classical Lie algebras:

\begin{split}\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/self_contraction_over_fundamental_trace}}}$}&=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left(\!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}\frac{({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}{+}2)}{({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}{+}1)}\!\right)^{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}n}}\!\!\big(1{-}\frac{1}{(-{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}{+}2))^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}n}-1}}\big)&\mathfrak{a}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}n}}\Big(1{+}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}(2{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}{+}1)}{(2{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k})^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}n}}}{+}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}(2{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}{+}3)}{({-}2{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k})^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}n}}}\Big)&\mathfrak{b}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}}\\
\frac{(2{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}{+}1)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}n}}}{2^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}n}}}\big(1{-}\frac{1{-}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}(1{+}({-}1)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}n}})}{(2{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}{+}1)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}n}{-}1}}\big)&\mathfrak{c}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}}\\
\frac{(2{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}{-}1)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}n}}}{2^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}n}}}\big(1{-}\frac{1{-}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}{+}1)(1{+}({-}1)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}n}})}{(2{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}{-}1)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}n}{-}1}}\big)&\mathfrak{d}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}}\\
\end{array}\right.\,.\end{split}\vskip-0.5pt

(4.20)

Single trace tensors with permuted orderings have non-vanishing overlap in general, but are generally sub-leading in the limit of large rank. Thus, these tensors
approach
colour-orthogonality in the large-rank limit
[
74
]
.

Beyond tree-level, it is easy to see that single-trace tensors do not suffice. But we may define
multi-trace
tensors in the natural way:

\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{R}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}\cdots}|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}b_{1}\cdots}|\cdots|{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}c_{1}\cdots})\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a_{1}\cdots})\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}b_{1}\cdots})\cdots\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.4}c_{1}\cdots})\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(4.21)

For each of the classical Lie algebras, Fierz identities (
4.33
) and (
4.35
) (derived in
section
4.3.1
below) allow one to systematically decompose any
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
-graph into multi-trace tensors involving the fundamental representation of the algebra; thus—even for
\mathfrak{d}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}}
gauge theories, for which these tensors are
incomplete
—these tensors necessarily span the subspace of tensors generated by the Feynman expansion for adjoint-coloured particles.

In the case of
\mathfrak{a}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}}
gauge theory, the Fierz identity (
4.34
) is easily seen to show that, in the expansion of
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
-graphs into single and multi-trace tensors, multi-traces are suppressed relative to single traces in the limit of large rank.

4.1.3
Feynman Diagrams involving Arbitrarily-Charged Fermions

Recall that the Feynman rules for charged matter must involve the generators of some representation of the Lie algebra associated with the gauge theory. Thus, for the scattering of charged matter with adjoints, Feynman diagrams are naturally endowed with colour tensors given by graphs of
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}
and
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}
vertices. Provided there is at least one charged Fermion in the process, the defining relation of a representation (
2.8
) allows one to eliminate all 3-point
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{ad}}
vertices, resulting in tensors built entirely from
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}
vertices.

Partial amplitudes involving arbitrary combinations of adjoint-coloured gluons and charged matter (all with colours given by the same (but arbitrary) representation) were studied by Melia in
[
75
,
76
,
77
]
, and the corresponding colour tensors at tree-level were described in
[
48
]
(see also
[
50
]
). These colour tensors can be viewed as analogues of the
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
-graphs described by DDM in
[
47
]
; and as with the
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}f}
-graphs, these tensors are far from orthogonal in colour-space.

We will have more to say on this subject in a forthcoming work
[
78
]
.

Finally, it is worthwhile to note that the issue of colour non-orthogonality has been addressed by several authors in recent years in the case of
\mathfrak{a}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}}
gauge theory. In particular, Clebsch-Gordan coefficients were used to define orthogonal colour tensors for processes in
\mathfrak{a}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}}
gauge theory in the works of
[
52
,
53
,
54
]
. These tensors formed what was called the ‘multiplet basis’, and can be viewed as a specialization of the general constructions we describe below.

4.2
Novel Colour Tensors for Scattering Adjoints and Fundamentals

Let us consider scattering amplitudes involving a single particle charged under any irreducible representation. The only possible colour structure consists of the identity tensor

\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}})\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\left\{\delta{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}}\right\}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(4.22)

This follows from Schur’s lemma, and the irreducibility of the representation.

For the scattering between any single particle charged under a representation
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}
and a gluon (or other particle transforming under the adjoint representation), the basis of colour tensors will consist of

\left\{\mathcal{B}_{\hskip-1.0pt\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}})\right\}_{\mu\in[m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}}]}\,\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip-4.0pt:\hskip 1.25pt}$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,\Big\{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[r]}}},\ldots\Big\}\;\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\Bigg\{\raisebox{5.0pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/rrad_clebsch_vertex}}}$}\Bigg\}\,,\vskip-0.5pt

(4.23)

where the multiplicity takes values in
\mu\!\in\![m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}R}}}}]
. Within this space of colour tensors, one can
always
be chosen to be the representation’s generators.

For the fundamental (or ‘defining’) representations ‘
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}F}}
’ defined in
appendix
B
, the multiplicity
m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}F}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{ad}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}F}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\mathbf{ad}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}F}}}}
is always 1, leaving us with a unique colour tensor associated with three-particle scattering:

\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}F}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}F}})\,\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,\Big\{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}F}}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[f]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[f]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}[f]}}}\Big\}\;\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\Bigg\{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-5.0pt}{\raisebox{9.125pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/ff_ad_clebsch}}}$}}\,\hskip 0.0pt\Bigg\}.\vskip-0.5pt

(4.24)

This uniqueness is shared for all representations of ‘fundamental’ Dynkin weight for each of the simple Lie algebras, but is not true for more general charged matter. We saw earlier that for
\mathfrak{a}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}>1}
Lie algebras,
m{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad\,ad}}}}_{\phantom{\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad\,ad}}}}\smash{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}}}\!=\!2
, leading to a space of two linearly independent colour tensors:

(\mathfrak{a}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}>1})
\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\hskip-1.0pt\mu}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}|\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}})\,\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,\Big\{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ad}}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}d}}{\hskip 0.0pt}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}_{\phantom{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}[\mathfrak{g}]}}}\Big\}\;\raisebox{-0.95pt}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\;\Bigg\{\raisebox{-5.0pt}{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{9.125pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/f_tensor_vertex}}}$}}\hskip 0.0pt,\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-5.0pt}{\raisebox{9.125pt}{$\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/d_tensor_vertex}}}$}}\,\hskip 0.0pt\Bigg\}.\hskip-150.0pt

(4.25)

Although the coefficient of the second colour tensor could be viewed as arising from an anomaly, both tensors contribute to bases of colour tensors constructed even for non-anomalous scattering of adjoints.

It is worth mentioning that for most all Lie algebras of rank
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}\!>\!1
, there exist irreducible representations which appear in tensor products with
arbitrarily large
multiplicities. For example, the
\mathbf{1024}
-dimensional representation of
\mathfrak{a}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}4}}
or the
\mathbf{4096}
-dimensional representation of
\mathfrak{d}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}4}}
(both with Dynkin labels
[1111]
) each require 4 independent colour tensors to describe its interactions with gluons. These novel colour tensors would not appear in the Feynman rules directly, but they could be generated perturbatively (or non-perturbatively).

4.3
Novel Colour Tensors for Four-Particle Scattering Amplitudes

In the completely general case of scattering four coloured particles, we have seen that a linearly-independent, orthogonal basis of colour tensors can be constructed as
